Formulas as Processes, Deadlock-Freedom as Choreographies (Extended Version)
Matteo Acclavio, Giulia Manara, Fabrizio Montesi

Abstract
We introduce a novel approach to studying properties of processes in the π \pi -calculus based on a processes-as-formulas interpretation, by establishing a correspondence between specific sequent calculus derivations and computation trees in the reduction semantics of the recursion-free π \pi -calculus. Our method provides a simple logical characterisation of deadlock-freedom for the recursion- and race-free fragment of the π \pi -calculus, supporting key features such as cyclic dependencies and an independence of the name restriction and parallel operators. Based on this technique, we establish a strong completeness result for a nontrivial choreographic language: all deadlock-free and race-free finite π \pi -calculus processes composed in parallel at the top level can be faithfully represented by a choreography. With these results, we show how the paradigm of computation-as-derivation extends the reach of logical methods for the study of concurrency, by bridging important gaps between logic, the expressiveness of the π \pi -calculus, and the expressiveness of choreographic languages.



1
Introduction

The Curry-Howard isomorphism is a remarkable example of the synergy between logic and programming languages, which establishes a
formulas-as-types
and
proofs-as-programs
(and
computation-as-reduction
) correspondences for functional programs
[
70
,
93
]
.
In view of this success, an analogous
proofs-as-processes
correspondence has been included in the agenda of the study of concurrent programming languages
[
1
,
17
]
.
The main idea in this research line is that,
as types provide a high-level specification of the input/output data types for a function, propositions in linear logic correspond to
session types

[
58
]
that specify the communication actions performed by processes (as in process calculi)
[
20
]
.
However, while the Curry-Howard correspondence fits functional programming naturally, it does not come without issues when applied to concurrency (we discuss the details in related work,
section

5
).
This is because functional programming deals with the ‘sequential’ aspect of computation (given an input, return a specific output), while most of the interesting aspects of concurrent computation are about the communication patterns used during the computation itself.
Therefore, as suggested in numerous works (e.g.,
[
72
,
75
,
10
,
10
,
12
]
), the Curry-Howard correspondence may not be the right lens for the study of concurrent programs.

In this paper we investigate an alternative proof-theoretical approach to the study of processes, which is based on logical operators that faithfully model the fundamental operators of process calculi (like prefixing, parallel, and restriction).
Our approach is close to the
computation-as-deduction
paradigm, where program executions are modelled as proof searches in a given sequent calculus; executions are therefore grounded in a logic by construction, as originally proposed by Miller in
[
72
]
.
Specifically, we interpret formulas as processes, inference rules as rules of an operational semantics, and (possibly partial) derivations as execution trees (snapshots of computations up to a certain point).

The approach we follow is not to be confused with the
intent of using logic as an auxiliary language to enunciate statements about computations, that is, viewing
computation-as-model
as done in Hennessy-Milner logic
[
53
,
56
]
, modal
\mu
-calculus
[
67
]
, Hoare logic
[
54
]
, or dynamic logics
[
50
,
9
]
.
We are instead interested in directly reasoning on programs and their execution using the language of the programs itself.
This allows for an immediate transfer of properties of proofs to properties of programs, without needing intermediate structures (e.g., models) or languages (e.g., types).

1.1
Contributions of the paper

We consider the recursion-free fragment of the
\pi
-calculus – as presented in
[
22
,
43
]
– and embed it in the language of the system
\mathsf{PiL}
from
[
8
]
.
\mathsf{PiL}
extends Girard’s first order multiplicative and additive linear logic
[
44
]
with a non-commutative and non-associative sequentiality connective (
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
), and nominal quantifiers (
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
and its dual
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}
) for variable scoping.

Using this embedding, we prove the following main results.

1.

We show that the operational semantics of the
\pi
-calculus is captured by the linear implication (
\multimap
) in
\mathsf{PiL}
: if we denote by
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
the formula encoding the process
P
, then

•

if
P
is a process reducing to
P^{\prime}
by performing a communication or an external choice, then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
; while

•

if
P
may reduce to
P_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,P_{\ell_{n}}
by performing an internal choice, then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}\left(\left[\!\left[P_{\ell_{i}}\right]\!\right]\right)\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

Crucial to prove this result is our proof that the system
\mathsf{PiL}
supports a substitution principle, which allows us to simulate reductions within a context.

2.

We establish a computation-as-deduction correspondence, which we use to characterise two key safety properties studied for race-free processes in terms of derivability in
\mathsf{PiL}
:

deadlock-freedom
, i.e., the property that a process can always keep executing until it eventually terminates
[
99
]
; and

progress
, i.e., the property that if a process gets stuck, it is always because of a missing interaction with an action that can be provided by the environment
[
29
]
.
1
1

1

For progress, in this paper we restrict our attention to processes that do not send restricted names.

In particular, thanks to the structure of
\mathsf{PiL}
and its operators, we can successfully detect safe processes that were previously problematic in the logical setting due to cyclic dependencies, like the process in
Equation

1
below.

\hskip-20.00003pt(\nu x)(\nu y)\left(x!\langle a\rangle.y\triangleleft\left\{\ell:y!\langle b\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\right\}\;|\;x?(a).y\triangleright\left\{\ell:y?(b).\mathsf{Nil},\ell^{\prime}:z!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\right\}\right)

(1)

3.

We show that our approach provides an adequate logical foundation for choreographic programming, a paradigm where programs are
choreographies
(coordination plans) that express the communications that a network of processes should enact
[
80
]
.
2
2

2

Networks are parallel compositions of sequential processes assigned to distinct locations.

Specifically, we establish a
choreographies-as-proofs
correspondence by using a sequent system, called
\mathsf{ChorL}
, that consists of rules derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

Our choreographies-as-proofs correspondence has an important consequence. An open question in theory of choreographic programming
[
81
]
is about how expressive choreographies can be:

What are the processes that can be captured by choreographies?

(2)

To date, there are no answers to this question for the setting of processes with unrestricted name mobility and cyclic dependencies.
Our correspondence implies a strong completeness result: in our setting, all and only race- and deadlock-free networks can be expressed as choreographies.
This is the first such completeness result in the case of recursion-free networks.

\hskip-10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{ccccc}P\mbox{ race-free deadlock-free }&\xRightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}&P\mbox{ {race-free} {deadlock-free} {flat} }\\
\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\left\Updownarrow\vphantom{\int}\right.{\mbox{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:deadlock}}}&&\qquad\qquad\displaystyle\left\Updownarrow\vphantom{\int}\right.{\mbox{\begin{tabular}[]{c}\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{cor:flatChor}\\
of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:deadlockfreeChor}\end{tabular}}}\\[-34.14322pt]
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]&\xRightarrow{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:ChorLderiv}}&\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{ChorL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]&\xLeftrightarrow{\mbox{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:proofsASchoreo}}}&\boxed{\mbox{\raisebox{28.45274pt}{\begin{tabular}[]{c}Exists a\\
choreography ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}$\\
such that\\
$\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\equiv P$\\[-20.0pt]
\end{tabular}}}}\end{array}

Road map of the main technical results in this work.

1.2
Structure of the paper

In
Section

2
we report
\mathsf{PiL}
and prove the additional technical results required for our development.
In
Section

3
we recall the syntax and semantics of the
\pi
-calculus, introduce an alternative reduction semantics with the same expressiveness with respect to the property of deadlock-freedom, show that the reduction semantics of the
\pi
-calculus is captured by linear implication in
\mathsf{PiL}
, and that each step of the reduction semantics of the
\pi
-calculus can be seen as blocks of rules in this system.
In
Section

4
we define our choreographic language and provide our completeness result for choreographies.
We discuss related work in
Section

5
.
We conclude in
Section

6
, where we discuss research directions opened by this work.
Due to space constraints, details of certain proofs are provided in the Appendix.

2
Non-commutative logic

In this section we recall
\mathsf{PiL}
and some of its established properties
[
8
]
. We then prove additional results required for our development.

2.1
The system
\mathsf{PiL}

Figure 2:
Formulas (with
x,y\in\mathcal{V}
), and their syntactic equivalences.

The language of
\mathsf{PiL}
is a first-order language containing the following.

•

Atoms generated by (i) a countable set of variables
\mathcal{V}
, (ii) a binary predicate on symbols
\langle-\oc-\rangle
, and (iii) its dual binary predicate
(-\wn-)
;

•

The multiplicative connectives for disjunction (
\invamp
) and conjunction (
\mathop{\otimes}
)
and
the additive connectives for disjunction (
\mathop{\oplus}
) and conjunction (
\with
) form
multiplicative additive linear logic

[
44
]
.
We generalize the standard binary
\mathop{\oplus}
and
\with
, allowing them to have any positive arity (including
1
) in order to avoid dealing with associativity and commutativity when modelling choices;

•

A binary non-commutative and non-associative self-dual multiplicative connective
precede
(
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
), whose properties reflect the ones of the prefix operator used in standard process calculi (see
CCS

[
77
]
and
\pi
-calculus
[
78
,
43
]
);

•

A unit (
\circ
). We observe that its properties reflect the ones of the terminated process
\mathsf{Nil}
.
Notably, it is the neutral element of the connectives that we will use to represent parallelism (
\invamp
) and sequentiality (
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
), and it is derivable from no assumption (thus also neutral element of
\mathop{\otimes}
);

•

The standard first order existential (
\exists
) and universal (
\forall
) quantifiers.

•

A
nominal quantifier

new
(
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
), and its dual
ya
(
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}
). We observe that these restrict variable scope in formulas in the same way that the
\nu
constructor in process calculi restricts the scope of names.

More precisely, we consider
formulas
generated by grammar in
Figure

2

modulo the standard
\alpha
-equivalence
from the same figure.

From now on, we assume formulas and sequents to be
clean
, that is, such that each variable
x\in\mathcal{V}
occurring in them can be bound by at most a unique universal quantifier or at most a pair of dual nominal quantifiers, and, if bound, it cannot occur free .
3
3

3

This can be considered as a variation of
Barendregt’s convention
.
It allows us to avoid variable renaming for universal and nominal quantifier rules in derivations, by assuming the bound variable to be the eigenvariable of the quantifier or the shared fresh name in the case of a pair of dual nominal quantifiers.

The
(linear) implication

A\multimap B
(resp. the
logical equivalence

A\multimapboth B
) is defined as
{A}^{\bot}\invamp B
(resp. as
(A\multimap B)\mathop{\otimes}(B\multimap A)
), where the
negation
(
{\cdot}^{\bot}
) is defined by the
de Morgan duality
in
Figure

2
.

The set
\mathsf{free}(A)
(resp.
\mathsf{free}(\Gamma)
) of
free variables
of a formula
A
(resp. of a sequent
\Gamma=A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
) is the set of atoms occurring in
A
which are not bound by any quantifier (resp. the set
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{free}(A_{i})
).
A
context
is a formula containing a single occurrence of a propositional variable
\bullet
(called
hole
).
We denote by
\mathcal{C}[A]\coloneqq\mathcal{C}\left[A/\bullet\right]
.
A
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}\invamp
-context
is a context
\mathcal{K}[\bullet]
of the form
\mathcal{K}[\bullet]=\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x_{1}}.\dots\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x_{n}}.([\bullet]\invamp A)
for a
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In this work we assume the reader to be familiar with the syntax of sequent calculus (see, e.g.,
[
96
]
), but we recall here the main definitions.

Notation 1

A
sequent
is a set of occurrences of formulas.

4
4

4

In a set of occurrences of formulas, it is assumed that each formula has a unique identifier, differently from a multiset of formulas where each formula has a multiplicity.
The former definition simplifies the process of tracing occurrences of formulas in a derivation, as we need in
Section

4
.

A
sequent rule

\mathsf{r}
with
premise
sequents
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
and
conclusion

\Gamma
is an expression of the form

\Gamma_{1}

\cdots

\Gamma_{n}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

.
A formula occurring in the conclusion (resp. in a premise) of a rule but in none of its premises (resp. not in its conclusion) is said
principal
(resp.
active
).
Given a set of rules
\mathsf{X}
, a
derivation
in
\mathsf{X}
is a non-empty tree
\mathcal{D}
of sequents, whose root is called
conclusion
,
such that every sequent occurring in
\mathcal{D}
is the conclusion of a rule in
\mathsf{X}
, whose children are (all and only) the premises of the rule.
An
open derivation
is a derivation whose leaves may be conclusions of no rules, in which case are called
open premises
.
We may denote a derivation (resp. an open derivation with a single open premise
\Delta
)
with conclusion
\Gamma
by

.
Finally we may write

\vdash\Gamma_{1}

\cdots

\vdash\Gamma_{n}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\vdash\Gamma

if there is an open derivation with premises
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
and conclusion
\Gamma
made only of rules
\mathsf{r}
.

A
nominal variable
is an element of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x^{\nabla}}
with
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
\nabla\in\{\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}},\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}\}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
is a set of nominal variables, we say that
x

occurs
in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x^{\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x^{\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}}}
is an element of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
.
A
(nominal) store

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
is a set of nominal variables such that each variable occurs at most once in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
.
A
judgement

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}}\vdash\Gamma
is a pair consisting of a clean sequent
\Gamma

and a store
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}
.
We write judgements
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}}\vdash\Gamma
with

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}=\varnothing

(resp.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x_{1}^{\nabla_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x_{n}^{\nabla_{n}}}\}
)
simply as

\vdash\Gamma
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x_{1}^{\nabla_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}x_{n}^{\nabla_{n}}}}}\vdash\Gamma
, i.e. omitting parenthesis).
We write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}_{2}
to denote the union of two stores such that a same variable does not occur in both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathcal{S}}_{2}
– i.e., a disjoint union.

The system
\mathsf{PiL}
is defined by the all rules in
Figure

3
except the rule
\mathsf{cut}
.
We write
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\Gamma
to denote that the judgement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\varnothing}\vdash\Gamma
is derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

Remark 1

The system
\mathsf{MLL}^{1}=\{\mathsf{ax},\invamp,\mathop{\otimes},\exists,\forall\}
is the standard one for first order multiplicative linear logic
[
44
]
. The rules
\mathop{\oplus}
and
\with
are generalisations of the standard ones in additive linear logic for the
n
-ary generalized connectives we consider here; thus, in proof search, the rule
\mathop{\oplus}
keeps only one
A_{k}
among all
A_{i}
occurring in
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
, and the rule
\with
branches the proof search in
n
premises. The rule
\mathsf{mix}
and
\circ
are standard for multiplicative linear logic with mix in presence of units
5
5

5

In presence of mix the two multiplicative units collapse.

[
41
,
28
]
, and the rule
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}^{\circ}
ensures that the unit
\circ
is not only the unit for the connectives
\invamp
and
\mathop{\otimes}
, but also for
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
.
The rule
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
is required to capture the self-duality of the connective
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
; it should be read as introducing at the same time the connective
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
and its dual (which in this case is
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
itself) – as a general underlying pattern for multiplicative connectives, see
[
2
, Remark 5]
.

The store is used to guarantee that each
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
is linked to at most a unique
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}
(or vice versa) in any branch of a derivation.
If a rule
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\circ}
(resp.
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}_{\circ}
) is applied, then the nominal quantifier is not linked, reason why the rule reminds the standard universal quantifier rule.
Otherwise, either the rule
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\mathsf{load}}
(resp.
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}_{\mathsf{load}}
) loads a nominal variable in the store, or a rule
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\mathsf{pop}}
(resp.
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}_{\mathsf{pop}}
) uses a nominal variable (of dual type) occurring in the store as a witness variable.
Note that in a derivation with the conclusion a judgement with empty store any

\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\mathsf{pop}}
(resp.
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}_{\mathsf{pop}}
) is uniquely linked to a
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\mathsf{load}}
(resp.
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}_{\mathsf{load}}
) below it.

4.47217pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\end{array}

Figure 3:

Sequent calculus rules, with
\dagger\coloneqq x\notin\mathsf{free}(\Gamma)
.

2.2
Proof Theoretical Properties of
\mathsf{PiL}

We now recall some basic proof-theoretical properties of the system
\mathsf{PiL}
and then prove additional results (
Theorem

2.2
) that will be important for the main technical results in this paper.

In
\mathsf{PiL}
we can prove that atomic axioms are sufficient to prove that the implication
A\multimap A
holds for any formula
A
.
Moreover, the
\mathsf{cut}
-rule is admissible in these systems, allowing us to conclude the transitivity of the linear implication, as well as the sub-formula property for all the rules of the systems.

Theorem 2.1
(
[
8
]
)

Let
\Gamma
be a non-empty sequent in
\mathsf{PiL}
.
Then

1.

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}{A}^{\bot},A
for any formula
A
;

2.

if
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}\cup\{\mathsf{cut}\}}\,}\Gamma
, then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\Gamma
;

3.

if
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap B
and
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}B\multimap C
, then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap C
.

Proposition 1
(
[
8
]
)

The following logical equivalences are derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
(for any
\sigma
permutation over
\{1,\ldots,n\}
).

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left(A\invamp\circ\right)&\multimapboth&A\\
\left(A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\circ\right)&\multimapboth&A\\
(A\invamp B)\invamp C&\multimapboth&A\invamp(B\invamp C)\\
A\invamp B&\multimapboth&B\invamp A\\
\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)&\multimapboth&\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{\sigma(i)}\right)\\
\left(\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)&\multimapboth&\left(\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}A_{\sigma(i)}\right)\end{array}&\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{y}.A)&\multimapboth&(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{y}.\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.A)\\
(\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.A_{i})&\multimapboth&(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\\
(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{A}._{i})&\multimapboth&(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-10.0pt]
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.(A\invamp D)&\multimapboth&(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.A)\invamp D\\
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.D&\multimapboth&D\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\mbox{if $x\notin\mathsf{free}(D)$}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\end{array}

(3)

Moreover,
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left(\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}(A_{i}\invamp B)\right)\multimap\left((\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\invamp B\right)
.

In addition to these properties, our development requires some new ones showing that implication is preserved in different contexts.
This is necessary because some rules in the operational semantics of the
\pi
-calculus enables rewriting of deeply nested subterms.
For this reason, we are required to establish properties similar to those for proving
subject reduction
in
\lambda
-calculus.
That is, we prove that in
\mathsf{PiL}
we can still reproduce the application of inference rules inside contexts preserving soundness and completeness.
These necessary properties are collected in the next theorem.

Theorem 2.2

For any context
\mathcal{C}[\bullet]
and
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}\invamp
-context
\mathcal{K}[\bullet]
we have:

1.

if
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap B
, then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\mathcal{C}[A]\multimap\mathcal{C}[B]
;

2.

if

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A_{i}\multimap B
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
then

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\bigwith_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}\left[A_{i}\right]\multimap\mathcal{K}[B]
;

3.

if

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap A^{\prime}
and

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\mathcal{C}[A^{\prime}]\multimap B
,
then

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\mathcal{C}[A]\multimap B
.

Proof

Item

1
and
Item

2
are proven by induction on the structure of the contexts.
To prove
Item

3
we use
Theorem

2.1
.
3
since if
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap A^{\prime}
, then by
1
also
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\mathcal{C}[A]\multimap\mathcal{C}[A^{\prime}]
.
See the full proof in
Appendix

0.A
.

Remark 2

With the same motivation, one might argue that a more natural way to establish a correspondence between proof search and execution of processes could be
deep inference

[
48
,
49
]
, since that would allow for applying rules to subformulas.
Here we chose to follow the more standard approach of sequent calculi, where rules can manipulate only top-level term-constructors (connectives).

Remark 3

In the proofs-as-processes interpretation,
\mathsf{cut}
is the linchpin that triggers the rewriting simulating the reduction semantics (cut elimination).
In our work, as in general in the study of processes-as-formulas, the
\mathsf{cut}
-rule is freed from being the keystone of the system.
Instead, the admissibility of the
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in the computation-as-deduction approach guarantees the existence of canonical models
[
74
,
73
,
55
]
.
In particular, we use this property to tame the syntactic bureaucracy of the reduction semantics due to rules
\mathsf{Par}
,
\mathsf{Res}
, and
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
, as well as to ensure the transitivity of logical implication (required to compose reduction steps).

3
Embedding the
\pi
-calculus in
\mathsf{PiL}

In this section we provide an interpretation of
\pi
-calculus processes as formulas in
\mathsf{PiL}
, showing also that each successful execution of a process corresponds to a branch in a correct derivation in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

We start by recalling the definition of the
\pi
-calculus and its operational semantics. Our presentation has explicit primitives for communicating choices, as usual in the literature of session types
[
98
,
99
,
66
]
.
We then present an alternative semantics in which we use structural precongruence instead of structural equivalence (this is a standard simplification
[
33
,
64
]
, which does not affect reasoning about deadlock-freedom, progress, or races).
We then provide a translation of processes
P
into formula
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
in
\mathsf{PiL}
and characterise deadlock-freedom for
P
in terms of provability of
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

3.1
The
\pi
-calculus and its reduction semantics

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\;}|@{\;}l@{\;}|@{\;}l@{\;}|@{\;}l}\lx@intercol\hfil\mbox{Processes}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\;&\mbox{Free names}&\mbox{bound names}\\
P,Q,R&\coloneqq&\mathsf{Nil}&\mbox{nil}&\varnothing&\varnothing\\
&|&x!\langle y\rangle.P&\mbox{send ($y$ on $x$)}&\mathcal{F}_{P}\setminus\{x,y\}&\mathcal{B}_{P}\\
&|&x?(y).P&\mbox{receive ($y$ on $x$)}&\mathcal{F}_{P}\setminus\{x\}&\mathcal{B}_{P}\cup\{y\}\\
&|&P\;|\;Q&\mbox{parallel}&\mathcal{F}_{P}\cup\mathcal{F}_{Q}&\mathcal{B}_{P}\cup\mathcal{B}_{Q}\\
&|&(\nu x)P&\mbox{nu}&\mathcal{F}_{P}\setminus\{x\}&\mathcal{B}_{P}\cup\{x\}\\
&|&x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{label send (on $x$)}&\bigcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{F}_{P_{\ell}}&\bigcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{B}_{P_{\ell}}\\
&|&x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{label receive (on $x$)}&\bigcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{F}_{P_{\ell}}&\bigcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{B}_{P_{\ell}}\end{array}

Figure 4:
Syntax for
\pi
-calculus processes with
x,y\in\mathcal{N}
and
L\subset\mathcal{L}
, and their sets of free and bound names.

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mbox{$\alpha$-equivalence}&\mbox{Structural equivalence generators}\\
\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}ll}\mathsf{Nil}&\equiv_{\alpha}&\mathsf{Nil}&\\
x?(y).P&\equiv_{\alpha}&x?(z).P\left[z/y\right]&\mbox{$z$ fresh for $P$}\\
x!\langle y\rangle.P&\equiv_{\alpha}&x!\langle y\rangle.Q&\mbox{if $P\equiv_{\alpha}Q$}\\
P\;|\;Q&\equiv_{\alpha}&R\;|\;S&\mbox{if $P\equiv_{\alpha}R$ and $Q\equiv_{\alpha}S$}\\
(\nu x)P&\equiv_{\alpha}&(\nu u)P\left[u/x\right]&\mbox{$u$ fresh for $P$}\\
x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\equiv_{\alpha}&x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{if $P_{\ell}\equiv_{\alpha}Q_{\ell}$ for all $\ell\in L$}\\
x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\equiv_{\alpha}&x\triangleright\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{if $P_{\ell}\equiv_{\alpha}Q_{\ell}$ for all $\ell\in L$}\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rcl}P\;|\;Q&{\Lleftarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow}&Q\;|\;P\\
(P\;|\;Q)\;|\;R&{\Lleftarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow}&P\;|\;(Q\;|\;R)\\
(\nu x)(\nu y)P&{\Lleftarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow}&(\nu y)(\nu x)P\\
\hline\cr P\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}&\Rrightarrow&P\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr(\nu x)S&\Rrightarrow&S\\
(\nu x)P\;|\;S&\Rrightarrow&(\nu x)(P\;|\;S)\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\mbox{ with $x\notin\mathsf{free}(S)$}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 5:

The standard
\alpha
-equivalence,
and relations generating of the structural equivalence (
\equiv
)
\pi
-calculus processes, where
A{\Lleftarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow}B
stands for
A\Rrightarrow B
and
B\Rrightarrow A
.

The set of
\pi
-calculus
processes
is generated by the grammar in
Figure

4
, which uses a fixed countable set of
(channel) names

\mathcal{N}=\{x,y,\ldots\}
and
a finite set of
labels

\mathcal{L}
.
We may denote by
(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})
a generic sequence
(\nu x_{1})\cdots(\nu x_{n})
of
\nu
-constructors of length
n>0
, and
we may simply write
x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}
(resp.
x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}
) as a shortcut for

x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
(resp.
x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
) whenever
L=\{\ell\}
.
A process is
sequential
if it contains no parallel (
\;|\;
) or restrictions (
\nu
), it is
flat
6
6

6

Sometimes referred to as
non hierarchical
in the literature.
if of the form
P=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(P_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;P_{n}\right)
for some sequential processes
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
(also called sequential components of
P
).
We use the common notation
P\left[x/y\right]
for substitution (recalled in
Figure

17
of
Appendix

0.A
).

The set
\mathcal{F}_{P}
of
free names
and the set
\mathcal{B}_{P}
of
bound names
in a process
P
are defined in
Figure

4
.
The set of
names
in
P
is denoted
\mathcal{N}_{P}
and a name
x
is
fresh
in
P
if
x\notin\mathcal{N}_{P}
.
A
context
is a process
\mathcal{P}[\bullet]
containing a single occurrence of a special free name
\bullet
called
hole
such that
\mathcal{P}[P]\coloneqq\mathcal{P}\left[P/\bullet\right]
is a process.
A
network context
is a context of the form
\mathcal{N}[\bullet]=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left([\bullet]\;|\;P_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;P_{n}\right)
.

The
\alpha
-equivalence
(
\equiv_{\alpha}
) is recalled in
Figure

5
.
To improve the presentation of the technical results, we assume processes written in an
unambiguous
form, that is, in such a way each bound variable
x\in\mathcal{B}_{P}
is bound by a unique
\nu
-constructor and do not occur free in
P
.
In the same figure we provide the relation
\Rrightarrow
, whose reflexive and transitive closure is denoted
\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow
, and we define the standard
(structural) equivalence
(
\equiv
) as the equivalence relation generated by the union of
\Rrightarrow
and
\equiv_{\alpha}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c|c}\begin{array}[]{r@{:\;}r@{\;\redsem\;}ll}\mathsf{Com}&x!\langle a\rangle.P\;|\;x?(b).Q\;\to&P\;|\;Q\left[a/b\right]&\\
\mathsf{Choice}&x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\;\to&x\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{k}:P_{\ell_{k}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\ell_{k}\in L\\
\mathsf{Label}&x\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{k}:P_{\ell_{k}}\right\}\;|\;x\triangleright\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\;\to&P_{\ell_{k}}\;|\;Q_{\ell_{k}}&\mbox{if }\ell_{k}\in L\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{r@{\;:\;}r@{\;\redsem\;}l@{\quad\mbox{if}\quad}l}\mathsf{Res}&(\nu x)P\;\to&(\nu x)P^{\prime}&P\to P^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{Par}&P\;|\;Q\;\to&P^{\prime}\;|\;Q&P\to P^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-5.0pt]
\mathsf{Struc}:\quad P\to Q\quad\mbox{if}\quad P\equiv P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}\equiv Q\end{array}

Figure 6:
Reduction semantics for the
\pi
-calculus.

The
reduction semantics
for processes is defined by the relation
\to
over processes induced by the rules in
Figure

6
.
As standard, we denote by
\twoheadrightarrow
the reflexive and transitive closure of
\to
.
As in
[
22
]
, to allow for nondeterminism, the syntax of processes contains a construct
x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
allowing for different options rather than the typical
x\triangleleft\left\{P:\ell\right\}
.
Thus, the corresponding rule
\mathsf{Choice}
for choosing among the available options induces a branching in the computation tree of the process.
We say that a process
P
is
stuck
if
P\not\equiv\mathsf{Nil}
and there is no
P^{\prime}
such that

P\to P^{\prime}
.
A process P is called
deadlock-free
if there is no stuck process
P^{\prime}
such that
P\twoheadrightarrow P^{\prime}
.
Also, a process
P
has
progress
7
7

7

See
Section

1.1
for the precise intended meaning of the term
progress
in this paper.

if it is deadlock-free or
P\;|\;Q
is deadlock-free for a stuck process
Q
.

Remark 4

Intuitively, deadlock-freedom means that there is always a part of a process that can reduce
[
64
,
81
]
.
Progress for processes, instead, was introduced in
[
29
]
to characterise processes that get stuck merely because they lack a communicating partner that could be provided by the environment.

For example, the process
P=(\nu x)(x!\langle a\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(b).\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;y!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil})
is not deadlock-free because it reduces (via
\mathsf{Com}
) to the stuck process
(\nu x)(\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;y!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil})\equiv y?(d).\mathsf{Nil}
, but this later has progress since
y!\langle d\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;y?(d).\mathsf{Nil}
is deadlock-free.

A process
P
has a
race condition
if there is a network context
\mathcal{N}[\bullet]
such that
P
is structurally equivalent to a term of the following shape.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c}\mathcal{N}[x!\langle y\rangle.R\;|\;x!\langle z\rangle.Q]&\mathcal{N}[x?(y).R\;|\;x?(z).Q]\\
\mathcal{N}[x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\;|\;x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}]&\mathcal{N}[x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\;|\;x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}]\end{array}

A process
P
is
race-free
if there is no
P^{\prime}
with a race condition such that
P\twoheadrightarrow P^{\prime}
.

Remark 5

Race conditions identify in a syntactic way the semantic property of a process
potentially
having nondeterministic executions because of concurrent actions on a same channel.
For example,
P=x!\langle a\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(b).\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(c).\mathsf{Nil}
has a race condition, and it can reduce either to

P_{b}=x?(b).\mathsf{Nil}
or to
P_{c}=x?(c).\mathsf{Nil}
according to the way the reduction rule
\mathsf{Com}
is applied.
We specify ‘potentially’ because, for example, the process
Q=(\nu x)\left(x?(b).\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(c).\mathsf{Nil}\right)
has a race but cannot reduce.
In fact, in the execution of a race-free process, rules
\mathsf{Com}
and
\mathsf{Label}
are applied deterministically.
That is, the same send (resp. selection) is synchronised via a
\mathsf{Com}
(resp. a
\mathsf{Label}
) with the same receive (resp. branching), and vice versa, in any possible (branch of an) execution.

3.2
A Simpler Equivalent Presentation of the Reduction Semantics

To simplify the presentation of the new methodologies we use in our new framework,
we replace the
structural equivalence
\equiv
with the
precongruence

\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow
(as in
[
64
,
33
]
).
In particular, such a precongruence orients the direction of scope extrusion (by extending the scope of the binder as much as possible), but also rules out those rewritings that may add superfluous information such as
P\Rrightarrow(P\;|\;\mathsf{Nil})
or
P\Rrightarrow((\nu x)P)
for a
x\notin\mathcal{N}_{P}
.
Thus in the reduction semantics we consider in this paper we employ the following rule instead of the standard
\mathsf{Struc}
(see
Figure

6
):

\begin{array}[]{cccccl}\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}:P\to Q&\mbox{if }&P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow P^{\prime}&=&\mathcal{P}[S]\to\mathcal{P}[S^{\prime}]&=Q\\
&\mbox{with}&P\neq P^{\prime}&\mbox{and}&S\to S^{\prime}&\mbox{ not via }\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}\end{array}

(4)

Remark 6

The reduction semantics using the rule
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
instead of
\mathsf{Struc}
is weaker because the set of processes reachable via a step of
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
is strictly contained in the set of processes reachable via
\mathsf{Struc}
.
By means of example, consider the process
x!\langle y\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(z).\mathsf{Nil}
which reduces to
both
\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}
and
\mathsf{Nil}
using
\mathsf{Struc}
, but can only reduce to
\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}
using
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
.

However, it is immediate to show that if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Struc}
, then there is a
Q\equiv P^{\prime}
such that
P\to Q
via
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
.
Therefore, the standard reduction semantics (containing the rule
\mathsf{Struc}
) is as informative as the one we consider here (where we use the rule
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
instead) for the study of deadlock-freedom and for the definition of the race condition.

In the definition of the rules of the reduction semantics,
the rules
\mathsf{Com}
,
\mathsf{Choice}
and
\mathsf{Label}
are, in some sense, performing ‘meaningful’ transformation on processes,
while rules
\mathsf{Res}
,
\mathsf{Par}
and
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
deal with the syntactic bureaucracy of rewriting modulo the structural equivalence.
In the proofs in the next sections we need to be able to identify in each reduction step
P\to P^{\prime}
the sub-process
\mathsf{rdx}_{(P,P^{\prime})}
of
P
(called
core-redex
) which is irreversibly transformed to the process
\mathsf{rdt}_{(P,P^{\prime})}
(called
core-reductum
), as well as to measure the amount of syntactical manipulations we need to ‘reach’ such a sub-process to apply a reduction step (which we call
entropy
). We make these concepts precise in the next definitions and exemplify them in
Figure

7
.

Definition 1

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
processes such that
P\to P^{\prime}
.
The
core

\mathsf{Core}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=(\mathsf{rdx}_{(P,P^{\prime})},\mathsf{rdt}_{(P,P^{\prime})})
and
the
entropy

\mathsf{Ent}_{(P,P^{\prime})}\in\mathbb{N}

of
P\to P^{\prime}
are defined as:

•

if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Com}
,
\mathsf{Label}
or
\mathsf{Choice}
, then

\mathsf{Ent}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=1
and

\mathsf{Core}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=(P,P^{\prime})
;

•

if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Par}
(resp.
\mathsf{Res}
),
then there are processes
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
Q\to Q^{\prime}
and a context
\mathcal{P}[\bullet]
of the form
\bullet\;|\;R
(resp. of the form
(\nu x)(\bullet)
) such that
P=\mathcal{P}[Q]
and
P^{\prime}=[Q^{\prime}]
by definition of the reduction step.
Then

\mathsf{Ent}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=2\mathsf{Ent}_{(Q,Q^{\prime})}

and

\mathsf{Core}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=\mathsf{Core}_{(Q,Q^{\prime})}
;

•

if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
, then there are processes
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow Q\to Q^{\prime}\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow P^{\prime}

with

P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow Q
and

Q^{\prime}\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow P^{\prime}
.
Then

\mathsf{Ent}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=3\mathsf{Ent}_{(Q,Q^{\prime})}

and

\mathsf{Core}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=\mathsf{Core}_{(Q,Q^{\prime})}
.

The
core-reduction
of
P\to P^{\prime}
is the rule used to reduce
\mathsf{rdx}_{(P,P^{\prime})}
to
\mathsf{rdt}_{(P,P^{\prime})}
.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\;}|@{\;}c@{\;}|@{\;}c@{\;}|@{\;}c@{\;}|@{\;}c}S&S^{\prime}&\mathsf{rdx}_{(S,S^{\prime})}&\mathsf{rdt}_{(S,S^{\prime})}&\mathsf{Ent}_{(S,S^{\prime})}\\
\hline\cr(x!\langle a\rangle.P\;|\;x?(y).Q)\;|\;R&(P\;|\;Q\left[a/y\right])\;|\;R&x!\langle a\rangle.P\;|\;x?(y).Q&P\;|\;Q\left[a/y\right]&2\\
\hline\cr(\nu a)\left(b!\langle a\rangle.P\right)\;|\;b?(c).R&(\nu a)\left(P\;|\;R\left[a/c\right]\right)&b!\langle a\rangle.P\;|\;b?(c).R&b!\langle a\rangle.P\;|\;b?(c).R&6\end{array}

Figure 7:
Examples of processes
S
and
S^{\prime}
such that
S\to S^{\prime}
, and the core-redex, core-reductum, and entropy of the rewriting step.

Definition 2

A
execution tree
of a process
P
is a tree of processes
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
with root
P
, such that a process
Q^{\prime}
is a child of
Q
if
Q\to Q^{\prime}
, and such that:

•

if the core-reduction of
Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Label}
, then
Q^{\prime}
is the unique child of
Q
;

•

if the core-reduction of
Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Choice}
, then the set
\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\}\ni Q^{\prime}
of children of
Q
is such that the core-reduction of
Q\to Q_{i}
is a
\mathsf{Choice}

and

\mathsf{rdx}_{(Q,Q_{i})}=\mathsf{rdx}_{(Q,Q^{\prime})}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

It is
maximal
if each leaf of the tree is a process
R\equiv\mathsf{Nil}
or is stuck.

We conclude this subsection with this result, which, together with
Remark

6
, allows us to consider each maximal execution tree as a witness of deadlock-freedom for race-free processes.

Lemma 1

Let
P
be a process. If
P
is deadlock-free, then each execution tree with root
P
can be extended to a maximal execution tree whose leaves are processes structurally equivalent to
\mathsf{Nil}
.

3.3
Translating Processes into Formulas

We define a translation of
\pi
-calculus processes into
\mathsf{PiL}
formulas.

Definition 3
(Processes as Formulas)

We associate to each
\pi
-calculus process
P
a formula
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
inductively defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l@{\qquad}r@{\;=\;}l@{\qquad}r@{\;=\;}l}\left[\!\left[\mathsf{Nil}\right]\!\right]&\circ&\left[\!\left[P\;|\;Q\right]\!\right]&\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]\invamp\left[\!\left[Q\right]\!\right]&\left[\!\left[(\nu x)(P)\right]\!\right]&\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]\\[5.0pt]
\left[\!\left[\bullet\right]\!\right]&\bullet&\left[\!\left[x!\langle y\rangle.P\right]\!\right]&\langle x\oc y\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]&\left[\!\left[x?(y).P\right]\!\right]&\exists{y}.\!\left((x\wn y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]\right)\end{array}\\[15.0pt]
\left[\!\left[x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right]\!\right]=\bigwith\limits_{\ell\in L}{\left(\langle x\oc\ell\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)}\qquad\left[\!\left[x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right]\!\right]=\bigoplus\limits_{\ell\in L}{\left((x\wn\ell)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)}\end{array}

(5)

Note that assuming
P
unambiguous, the translation is a clean formula.

Remark 7

The reader familiar with session types could be curious about the choice of representing by a
\with
-formula a process of the form
x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
(whose session type is a
\mathop{\oplus}
-type)
and, dually, by a
\mathop{\oplus}
-formula a process
x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
(whose session type is a
\with
-type).
This is only an apparent contradiction because
our formulas are not types
. Rather, they encode processes whose executions are then derivations in the
\mathsf{PiL}
system.
Under this new interpretation, during proof search the rule for
\with
gives exactly the expected branching of possible executions of terms like
x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}
, corresponding to rule
\mathsf{Choice}
in the reduction semantics.
Rule
\mathsf{Label}
can then be applied ‘afterwards’ (above in the derivation) to select the appropriate branch at the receiver, discarding all the others. Thus, in the formulas-as-processes, receiving a label corresponds to
\oplus
.

For the same reason, parallel composition is represented by
\invamp
(as in
[
74
,
13
]
), while in most works using propositions as session types it is represented by
\mathsf{cut}
and
\mathop{\otimes}
.
We will come back to this aspect in
Section

5
.

Proposition 2

Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
processes.
If
P_{1}\Rrightarrow P_{2}
then
\left[\!\left[P_{2}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P_{1}\right]\!\right]
.

Proof

\left[\!\left[P\;|\;Q\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\left[\!\left[Q\;|\;P\right]\!\right]
and
\left[\!\left[(P\;|\;Q)\;|\;R\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\left[\!\left[P\;|\;(Q\;|\;R)\right]\!\right]
derive from commutativity and associativity of
\invamp
(see
Proposition

1
).
The logical equivalences
\left[\!\left[P\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
and
\left[\!\left[(\nu x)\mathsf{Nil}\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\left[\!\left[\mathsf{Nil}\right]\!\right]

are direct consequence of the ones in
Figure

2
.
The implication
\left[\!\left[(\nu x)(P\;|\;Q)\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[(\nu x)P\;|\;Q\right]\!\right]
for
x\notin\mathsf{free}(Q)
is shown in
Proposition

1
.
Finally,
\left[\!\left[(\nu x)(\nu y)P\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\left[\!\left[(\nu y)(\nu x)P\right]\!\right]
derives from the quantifier shifts

\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{y}.P=\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{y}.\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.P
(
Figure

2
).

Figure 8:

Derivations in
\mathsf{PiL}
corresponding to the rules
\mathsf{Com}
,
\mathsf{Choice}
and
\mathsf{Label}
of the reduction semantics of the
\pi
-calculus.

We can now establish a correspondence between process reductions and linear implication in
\mathsf{PiL}
,
as well as a correspondence between each computation tree with root a process
P
and a proof search strategy in
\mathsf{PiL}
for the formula
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.
Combining these two results, we obtain a purely logical characterisation of deadlock-free processes as pre-images via
\left[\!\left[\cdot\right]\!\right]
of formulas derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

Lemma 2

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
processes.

1.

If
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow P^{\prime}
, then
\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

2.

If
P\to P^{\prime}
, then either

(a)

the core-reduction of
P\to P^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Label}
, and

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
;

(b)

or the core-reduction of
P\to P^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Choice}
then
there is a set
\{P_{\ell}\mid\ell\in L\}\ni P^{\prime}

such that

P\to P_{\ell}

for all
\ell\in L
and

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left(\bigwith_{\ell\in L}\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

Proof

Item

1
is proven using
Proposition

1

and transitivity of
\multimap
(see
Theorem

2.1
.
3
).
To prove
Item

2
we reason by induction on entropy:

•

if
\mathsf{Ent}_{(P,P^{\prime})}=1
then
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Com}
,
\mathsf{Label}
or
\mathsf{Choice}
and we conclude using the derivations in
Figure

8
;

•

if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Par}
(resp.
\mathsf{Res}
),
then there is a context
\mathcal{P}[\bullet]=\left(\bullet\;|\;R\right)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}[\bullet]=(\nu x)\bullet
) such that

P=\mathcal{P}[S]
and
P^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}[S^{\prime}]
.
We conclude using
Theorem

2.2
.
1
and
Theorem

2.1
.
3
;

•

if
P\to P^{\prime}
via
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
, then there is
S
such that
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow S
and
S\to P^{\prime}
(via a rule different from
\mathsf{Struc}^{\Rrightarrow}
).
We conclude by
Theorem

2.1
.
3
using
Item

1
and
Theorem

2.2
2
.

Using this lemma we can prove the correspondence between deadlock-freedom and derivability in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
P
be a race-free process.
Then
P
is deadlock-free iff
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.
More precisely,
\mathord{\vdash_{\{\circ,\mathsf{ax},\invamp,\mathsf{mix},\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{581838}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{581838}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{406017}{194232}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{327520}{138737}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}},\with,\mathop{\oplus},\exists,\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}^{\circ}\}}\,}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

Proof

It suffices to establish a correspondence between maximal execution trees and derivations in
\mathsf{PiL}
.
Details are provided in the appendix.

(
\Rightarrow
)

If
P
is deadlock-free, then, any maximal execution tree
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
with root
P
has leaves which are processes structurally equivalent to
\mathsf{Nil}
by
Lemma

1
.
By induction on the structure of
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
, we can define a derivation in
\mathsf{PiL}\cup\{\mathsf{cut}\}
composing (using
\mathsf{cut}
) the derivations allowing simulating the transitions of the reduction semantics (
Lemma

2
);
thus we obtain a derivation in
\mathsf{PiL}
by applying cut-elimination (
Theorem

2.1
.
2
).
We conclude observing that the subformula property ensures that only rules in such cut-free derivation are in
\{\circ,\mathsf{ax},\invamp,\mathsf{mix},\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}},\with,\mathop{\oplus},\exists,\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}^{\circ}\}
.
Note that all judgements in such a derivation are empty.

(
\Leftarrow
)

To prove the converse, we show that each derivation
\mathcal{D}_{P}
of
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
can be transformed using the
rule permutations
in
Figure

9
into a derivation
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}_{P}}
made of blocks of rules consisting of sequences of
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
- and
\invamp
-rules only, or blocks as the ones shown in
Equation

6
below.

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

\vdash\langle x\oc y\rangle,(x\wn y)

\vdash A,B\left[y/z\right],\Gamma

\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{581838}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{581838}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{406017}{194232}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{327520}{138737}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\;

\vdash\langle x\oc y\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}A,(x\wn y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}B\left[y/z\right],\Gamma

\scriptstyle\exists\;

identifies an application of a
\mathsf{Com}
(resp. a
\mathsf{Bra}
followed by a
\mathsf{Sel}
).

Note that since
P
is race-free, then it suffices to reason on a single execution tree and not to take into account all possible execution trees of
P
.

Corollary 1

Let
P
be a race-free process.
Then

P
has progress iff
there is a
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}\invamp
-context
\mathcal{C}[\bullet]
such that

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\mathcal{C}[P]
.
