Figure 9:

Rule permutations
with

\mathsf{r}^{1},\mathsf{r}^{1}_{1},\mathsf{r}^{1}_{2}\in\{\invamp,\exists,\mathop{\oplus},\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}_{\circ}\}

and

\mathsf{r}^{2},\mathsf{r}^{2}_{1},\mathsf{r}^{2}_{2}\in\{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}},\mathop{\otimes},\mathsf{mix}\}
.

We conclude this section by showing that progress for processes which never send ‘private’ channels can be easily captured in this new setting.
Specifically, we say that a process
P
has
private mobility
if it is of the form
P=\mathcal{P}[a!\langle x\rangle]
for an
a
bound by a
\nu
in
\mathcal{P}
.
We also denote by
\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
the formula obtained by replacing with a unit (
\circ
) any atom in
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
of the form
\langle x\oc y\rangle
or
(x\wn y)
for any
x\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
.

Theorem 3.2

Let
P
be a race-free process without private mobility.
Then

P
has progress iff

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\partial_{\mathcal{F}_{P}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

Proof

We prove a simulation result (as
Lemma

2
) for
\partial_{\mathcal{F}_{P}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
, and we conclude with the same argument used in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.
It
P
is deadlock-free, then we conclude as in
Theorem

3.1
.
Otherwise, since
P
has progress there is
Q
such that
P\;|\;Q
is deadlock-free.
By definition, we must have that

\mathcal{N}_{Q}=\mathcal{F}_{Q}
(otherwise either
Q
is not stuck or
P\;|\;Q
is not deadlock-free)
and that

\mathcal{F}_{Q}=\mathcal{F}_{P}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
Thus
\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P\;|\;Q\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
by the fact that
\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[Q\right]\!\right]
contains no atoms (i.e., only units) and
\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}A\multimapboth A\multimapboth\circ\invamp A
(see
Proposition

1
).

•

If
P\;|\;Q
is deadlock-free for
Q
stuck and
P\;|\;Q\to R
via
\mathsf{Par}

then,
R=P^{\prime}\;|\;Q
and
P\to P^{\prime}
.
If the core-reduction of
P\twoheadrightarrow P^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Label}
,
then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
;
if the core-reduction is a
\mathsf{Choice}
,
there is a set of processes
\{P_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\ni P^{\prime}
such that

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left(\bigwith_{\ell\in L}\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)\multimap\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.
This is proven by induction on the entropy as in
Lemma

2
.

•

If
P^{\prime}\;|\;Q\to P^{\prime}
not via
\mathsf{Par}
and then the core-reduction is either a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Sel}
.
In this case can prove as that

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]
because
\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\;|\;Q\right]\!\right]\multimapboth\partial_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}}\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]
.

Remark 8

To understand the requirement on private mobility in
Theorem

3.2
, consider the process
P=(\nu a)(b!\langle a\rangle.a!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil})
. This process has progress, because

(P\;|\;b?(x).x?(c).\mathsf{Nil})\equiv(\nu a)(b!\langle a\rangle.a!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;b?(x).x?(c).\mathsf{Nil})\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{Nil}\;.

However,

\partial_{\{b\}}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]=\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{a}.\!\left(\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\langle a\oc c\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\circ\right)

is not derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
.
This makes our characterisation of progress as powerful as in previous work
[
22
]

(where the condition is not made explicit but clearly necessary, see the definition of ‘co-process’ therein).

4
Completeness of Choreographies

In this section we prove that any deadlock-free flat process can be expressed as a choreography, as intended in the paradigm of choreographic programming
[
80
]
.
Key to this result is establishing a
proofs-as-choreographies
correspondence, whereby choreographies can be seen as derivations in the
\mathsf{PiL}
system.

To this end, we first introduce the syntax and semantics of
choreographies
, the typical accompanying language for describing their implementations in terms of located processes (the
endpoint calculus
), and a notion of endpoint projection (EPP) from choreographies to processes.
We then define the sequent calculus
\mathsf{ChorL}
operating on sequents in which (occurrences of) formulas are labelled by process names, and we conclude by establishing the proofs-as-choreographies correspondence.

4.1
Choreographies

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Choreographies }\\
\begin{array}[]{rccccccl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}\mbox{ (with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}$ containing no $(\nu x)$)}\\
&\mbox{end}&&\mbox{communication}&&\mbox{choice}&&\mbox{restriction}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mbox{Reduction semantics for Choreographies}\\
\begin{array}[]{lcr@{\;}c@{\;}ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\left[x/y\right]\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Choice}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}:k}\;\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{i}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{C}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\mbox{for a }\ell_{i}\in L\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{i}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{C}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell_{i}}&\mbox{if }\ell_{i}\in L^{\prime}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Rest}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}&\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}&\mbox{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Com}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}&\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}^{\prime}\;\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}&\mbox{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Choice}}&:&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}^{\prime}\;\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}&\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}^{\prime}\\
\mbox{for all $\ell\in L$}\end{array}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\mbox{with }\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}^{\prime})\cap\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}},k\}=\varnothing\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10:
Syntax and semantics for choreographies, where

{\mathsf{p}}
and
{\mathsf{q}}
are distinct process names in
\mathcal{P}
,

x,y\in\mathcal{V}
,

L\subseteq L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
,
and

S_{\ell}
are sequential processes (see
Remark

9
).

In a choreographic language, terms (called
choreographies
) are coordination plans that express the overall behaviour of a network of processes
[
81
]
.
The
choreographies
that we consider in this paper are generated by a set of
process names

\mathcal{P}
, a set of variables
\mathcal{V}
, and a set of
selection labels

\mathcal{L}
as shown in
Figure

10
.
A choreography can be either:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}
, the terminated choreography;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};C
, a communication from a process
{\mathsf{p}}
to another
{\mathsf{q}}
with a continuation
C
(
y
is bound in
C
and can appear only under
{\mathsf{q}}
);

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}
, a choice by a process
{\mathsf{p}}
of a particular branch
L
offered by another process
{\mathsf{q}}
8
8

8

The set
L^{\prime}
of labels the process
{\mathsf{q}}
can accept contains the set
L
of labels
{\mathsf{p}}
can send.
For the continuation of labels in
L^{\prime}\setminus L
we only allow sequential processes because we do not allow nested parallel in the target language of the projection (see next subsection).

; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}
, which restricts
x
in a choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}
in which the variable
x
always occur free (i.e., no
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}{}
occurs in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}
).
9
9

9

By allowing the construct
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x)}
only in the case in which
x
is not bound in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{x}
, we ensure that choreographies are always written using
Barendregt’s convention
.
This means that each variable
x
can be bound by at most one restriction
\nu
, and
x
cannot appear both free and bound in a choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
.
As a result, we can adopt a lighter labelling discipline for the reduction semantics compared to the one used in
[
25
]
– see the rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Rest}}
in
Figure

10
.

Note that we consider communication of process names or variables only (that is,
k\in\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{V}
).
We say that a choreography is
flat
if it is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\mathsf{rf}}}
for a
restriction-free
(i.e., containing no occurrences of
\nu
) choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\mathsf{rf}}}
.
In the same figure we also provide the
reduction semantics
of our choreographic language, where each reduction step is labelled by a
reduction label

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}
from the following set.

\left\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}\;,\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}:k}\mid{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}}\in\mathcal{P},\;k\in\mathcal{V}\right\}

(7)

To each reduction label
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}
we associate the set
\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu})
of processes names and variables occurring in it – i.e.
\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k})=\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}},k\}
and
\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}:k})=\{{\mathsf{p}},k\}
.

In the semantics,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}
executes a communication while
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Choice}}
allows a process
{\mathsf{p}}
to make an internal choice.
Rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}
then communicates a label from
{\mathsf{p}}
to
{\mathsf{q}}
, which then continue with the choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}
(but never with a sequential process
S_{\ell}
, see
Remark

9
).
Rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Rest}}
lifts reductions under restrictions.
Lastly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Com}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Choice}}
) models the standard out-of-order execution of independent communications that can be reduced by rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}
(resp. both rules
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Choice}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}
) – this is the choreographic equivalent of parallel composition in process calculi
[
81
]
.

Example 1

The next choreography expresses the communication behaviour of the processes given in
Equation

1
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{a}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{a}:x};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{{\{\ell\}}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{{\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}}}:y}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{b}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{b}:y};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}\\
\ell^{\prime}\colon z!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\end{array}\right\}}

(8)

It can be executed by applying rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}
and then rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}
.
Note that we do not need to use rule
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Choice}}
before applying
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}
, because the set of labels
L
in the choice constructor is a singleton.

Remark 9

From the programmer’s viewpoint, choice instructions may contain some unnecessary information since no label
\ell^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}\setminus L
will never be selected during the execution of a choreography – and thus no continuation will execute the process
S_{\ell^{\prime}}
.
This ‘garbage’ code is typical of works on choreographies and logic
[
26
,
21
]
, and we share the same motivation: we want to be able to capture the entire flat fragment of the
\pi
-calculus, where such garbage code cannot be prohibited.
For example, without garbage code, the choreography in
Equation

8
would not be a complete representation of the endpoint process in
Equation

10
(see also
Equation

1
).

4.2
Endpoint Projection

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Structural Equivalence}\\
{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;P\;\equiv\;P\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\nu x_{1})\cdots(\nu x_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\;\equiv\;(\nu x_{\tau(1)})\cdot(\nu x_{\tau(k)})\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{\sigma(i)}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{\sigma(i)}\\
\mbox{for any $\sigma$ permutation over $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $\tau$ over $\{1,\ldots,k\}$}\\
\hline\cr\\[-10.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Reduction Semantics }\\
\begin{array}[]{l@{\;:\;}rcll}\mathsf{E}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Com}&\mathcal{N}[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k!\langle x\rangle.S\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k?(y).S^{\prime}]&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}&\mathcal{N}[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon S\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon S^{\prime}\left[x/y\right]]\\
\mathsf{E}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Choice}&\mathcal{N}\left[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell:S_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right]&\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}:k}\;\;}&\mathcal{N}\left[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{i}:S_{\ell_{i}}\right\}\right]&\mbox{ for each }\ell_{i}\in L\\
\mathsf{E}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Label}&\mathcal{N}\left[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{i}:S_{\ell_{i}}\right\}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleright\left\{\ell:S^{\prime}_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right]&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}&\mathcal{N}\left[{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{\ell_{i}}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon S^{\prime}_{\ell_{i}}\right]&\mbox{ if }\ell_{i}\in L\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 11:

Simplified presentation of the structural equivalence and reduction semantics for the endpoint calculus,
where
\mathcal{N}\left[P\right]\equiv(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(P\;|\;\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon T_{i}\right)
.

Our choreographies can be mechanically translated into processes via the standard technique of endpoint projection (EPP)
[
81
]
.
To simplify the presentation of projection, we adopt the standard convention of enriching the language of processes with process names labelling each sequential component of a flat process
[
52
,
81
]
.
That is, a flat process of the form
(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(S_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;S_{n}\right)
is represented by an
endpoint process

(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left({\mathsf{p}}_{1}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}_{n}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{n}\right)\quad\mbox{or}\quad(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\right)

(9)

where all names
{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{p}}_{n}
are distinct.
10
10

10

In the literature, a process
P
with name
{\mathsf{p}}
is usually written
{\mathsf{p}}[P]

[
52
,
81
]
. We adopt the alternative writing
{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon P
to avoid confusion with the notation used for contexts.

The process calculus over these processes is dubbed
endpoint calculus
.

The definition of
endpoint projection
is provided by the partial function
\mathsf{EPP}
in
Figure

12
(it is defined only for flat choreographies, as in
[
25
]
).
It is a straightforward adaptation to our syntax of the textbook presentation of projection
[
81
]
. In particular, it uses a
merge
operator
\sqcup
(originally from
[
23
]
) to support propagation of knowledge about choices. That is, if a process
\mathsf{r}
needs to behave differently in two branches of a choice communicated from
{\mathsf{p}}
to
{\mathsf{q}}
, it can do so by receiving different labels in these two branches. Merge then produces a term for
\mathsf{r}
that behaves as prescribed by the first (respectively the second) branch when it receives the first (respectively the second) label.
If
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
is defined for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
we say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
is
projectable
.

Example 2

The EPP of the choreography in
Equation

8
is

(\nu x)(\nu y)\left({\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon x!\langle a\rangle.y\triangleleft\left\{\ell:y!\langle b\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\right\}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon x?(a).y\triangleright\left\{\ell:y?(b).\mathsf{Nil},\ell^{\prime}:z!\langle c\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\right\}\right)

(10)

which is precisely the one in
Equation

1
annotated with process names.

Structural equivalence and reduction semantics for the endpoint calculus are obtained by the one of the
\pi
-calculus assuming that each structural equivalence (
\equiv
) and reduction step (
\to
) preserves the process names.
Note that, for the purpose of studying deadlock-freedom, structural equivalence and reduction rules can be simplified as shown in
Figure

11
.
Each reduction step is labelled by the same labels used in the reduction semantics of choreographies (see
Equation

7
), allowing us to retain the information about which sequential components and channel are involved in each reduction step.

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\begin{cases}(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\mathsf{rf}}}\right)&\mbox{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\mathsf{rf}}}$ with ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\mathsf{rf}}}$ restriction-free}\\
{\mathsf{p}}_{0}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{Nil}&\mbox{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}\mbox{ (for a given ${\mathsf{p}}_{0}$)}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{1}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\;|\;\cdots\;|\;\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{n}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&\mbox{otherwise, with ${\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{p}}_{n}$ all process names in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}$}\end{cases}\\[10.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-8.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{r@{=}l}\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}\right)=\mathsf{Nil}\qquad\qquad\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S}\right)&\begin{cases}\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S}\right)&\mbox{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S}$ is a choreography }\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\right)&\mbox{if
${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}$ is a process }\end{cases}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&\begin{cases}k!\langle x\rangle.\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}={\mathsf{p}}\\
k?(y).\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}={\mathsf{q}}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\notin\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}}\}\end{cases}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}\right)&\begin{cases}k\triangleleft\left\{\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}={\mathsf{p}}\\
k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\
\ell:S_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}={\mathsf{q}}\\
\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\mbox{if }{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\notin\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}}\}\end{cases}\end{array}\\
\\[-5.0pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\\[-5.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{c}\big((\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\right)\big)\bigsqcup\big((\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon T_{i}\right)\big)=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}\right)\\[10.0pt]
\mathsf{Nil}\sqcup\mathsf{Nil}=\mathsf{Nil}\qquad(x!\langle y\rangle.T)\bigsqcup(x!\langle y\rangle.S)=x!\langle y\rangle.T\sqcup S\qquad(x?(y).T)\bigsqcup(x?(y).S)=x?(y).T\sqcup S\\[8.0pt]
\left(x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right)\bigsqcup\left(x\triangleright\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\right)=x\triangleright\left(\{\ell:P_{\ell}\sqcup Q_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L\cap L^{\prime}}\cup\{\ell:P_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L\setminus L^{\prime}}\cup\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L}\right)\\[8.0pt]
\mbox{ only if $L=L^{\prime}$ : }\left(x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right)\bigsqcup\left(x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\right)=x\triangleleft\{\ell:P_{\ell}\sqcup Q_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in L}\end{array}\end{array}

Notation 2

If
P
and
Q
are endpoint processes, we write
P\sqsupseteq Q
iff
P\sqcup Q=P
.

Theorem 4.1

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
be a projectable flat choreography.

•

Completeness
: if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}P\sqsupseteq\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)
;

•

Soundness
:
if
P\sqsupseteq\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
and
P\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}P^{\prime}
, then there is a choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)
.

Proof

The proof is obtained by adapting the proof provided in, e.g.,
[
80
,
81
,
82
]
to the
language we consider in this paper. Details are given in
Appendix

0.A
.

4.3
A Sequent Calculus for the Endpoint Calculus

To establish the correspondence between proofs of (formulas encoding) endpoint processes and choreographies, we enrich the syntax of formulas by adding labels (on sub-formulas) carrying the same information of the process names used in the syntax of the endpoint calculus.
More precisely, we consider a translation
\left[\!\left[\cdot\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
from endpoint processes to
annotated formulas
of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left(A\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}}}
.

Definition 4

For any endpoint process
P=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\right)
we define the formula
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}=\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x_{1}}.\dots\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x_{n}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left(\left[\!\left[S_{1}\right]\!\right]\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{1}}}\invamp\cdots\invamp{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left(\left[\!\left[S_{n}\right]\!\right]\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{n}}}
, and the sequent
\left\lfloor\!\left\lfloor P\right\rfloor\!\right\rfloor={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left(\left[\!\left[S_{1}\right]\!\right]\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\left(\left[\!\left[S_{n}\right]\!\right]\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{n}}}
.

Note that because of the subformula property
11
11

11

Assuming an initial
\alpha
-renaming on
P
such that
P
is unambiguous, and such each variable bound by a receive action is the same as the unique (because of race-freedom) variable sent by the matching send action.
For example, we would write
x!\langle a\rangle\;|\;x?(a)
instead of
x!\langle a\rangle\;|\;x?(b)
.

of rules in
\mathsf{PiL}
, such labelling can be propagated in a derivation by labelling each active formula of a rule (which is a sub-formula of one of the principal formulas of the rule) with the same process name of the corresponding active formula.

\mathsf{PiL}

Figure 14:
Derivability (in
\mathsf{PiL}
) of the rules in
\mathsf{ChorL}
.

We now introduce a sequent calculus for the endpoint calculus, given in
Figure

13
, which consists purely of rules that are derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
. That is, for every rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathsf{ChorL}
there is an open derivation in
\mathsf{PiL}
with the same open premises and conclusion as
\mathsf{r}
.

Lemma 3

Each rule in
\mathsf{ChorL}
is derivable in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

Moreover, if the premise (resp. conclusion) of a rule in
\mathsf{ChorL}
is of the form
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
, then its conclusion is so (resp. all its premise are so).

Proof

Derivations with the same premise(s) and conclusion of a rule in
\mathsf{ChorL}
are shown in
Figure

14
.
The second part of the statement follows by rule inspection.

We can refine
Theorem

3.1
for race-free flat processes thanks to the fact that in endpoint processes parallel and restrictions can only occur at the top level.
This allows us to consider derivations in
\mathsf{PiL}
made of blocks of rule applications as those in
Figure

14
, each corresponding to a single instance of a rule in
\mathsf{ChorL}
.

Theorem 4.2

A race-free endpoint process
P
is deadlock-free iff
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{ChorL}}\,}\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof

If
P=(\nu x_{1}\dots x_{k})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon S_{i}\right)
is deadlock-free,
then by
Theorem

3.1
there is a derivation
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
in
\mathsf{PiL}
with conclusion

\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
.
Using rule permutations,
we can transform
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
into a derivation made (bottom-up) of possibly some
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
-rules followed by
\invamp
-rules (i.e., an open derivation of the same shape of
\mathcal{D}_{\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\rangle}
or
\mathcal{D}_{\varnothing}
from
Figure

14
), followed by a derivation
\mathcal{D}
of the sequent
\left[\!\left[P_{1}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}},\ldots,\left[\!\left[P_{n}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
which is organized in blocks of rules as open derivations in
Figure

14
.
Note that derivations of the form
\mathcal{D}_{\langle{\mathsf{p}},x,{\mathsf{q}},y,k\rangle}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\langle{\mathsf{p}},L,{\mathsf{q}},L^{\prime},k\rangle}
) correspond to the open derivations
in
Equation

6
.
Thus we can replace
\mathcal{D}_{\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\rangle}
or
\mathcal{D}_{\varnothing}
by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{flat}
,
each

\mathcal{D}_{\langle{\mathsf{p}},x,{\mathsf{q}},y,k\rangle}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\langle{\mathsf{p}},L,{\mathsf{q}},L^{\prime},k\rangle}
)
by a

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Com}
(resp. by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Sel}
),
and
each
\mathcal{D}_{n}
with a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Init}
,
obtaining the desired derivation in
\mathsf{ChorL}
.

The converse is a consequence of
Lemma

3
and
Theorem

3.1
.

k\oc\ell\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[T_{\ell}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}\right)}\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.16}{\left(\bigoplus\limits_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}{\left((k\wn\ell)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[T^{\prime}_{\ell}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}\right)}\right)}_{{\mathsf{q}}}}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 56.67996pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\right)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Chor}{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 35.21759pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 12.2847pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\kern 3.0pt\hrule width=4.2pt\vss}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.20001pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern-1.43518pt\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\;$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox to8.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern-1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 47.50229pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.1}{\left(\left[\!\left[T_{\ell}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}\right)}_{{\mathsf{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.16}{\left(\left[\!\left[T^{\prime}_{\ell}\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}\right)}_{{\mathsf{q}}}}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\right)}}}}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{T^{\prime}_{\ell}}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}\end{array}

Figure 15:
Interpretation of a derivation in
\mathsf{ChorL}
as a choreography.

4.4
Proofs as Choreographies

We can now prove a completeness result of choreographies with respect to the set of deadlock-free flat processes: each deadlock-free flat process is the EPP of a (flat) choreography.
To prove this result, we rely on
Theorem

4.2
to establish a direct correspondence between derivations of a sequent encoding a race-free endpoint process in the sequent system
\mathsf{ChorL}
, and execution trees of the same endpoint process.
In
Appendix

0.B
we provide an example the process of choreography extraction from a derivation in
\mathsf{ChorL}
of a deadlock-free endpoint process.

Theorem 4.3

Let
P
be a race-free endpoint process.
Then

\mbox{$P$ is deadlock-free }\iff\mbox{there is a choreography ${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}$ such that $\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=P$.}

Proof

If
P
is deadlock-free, then by
Theorem

4.2
there is a derivation in
\mathsf{ChorL}
with conclusion
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]^{\mathcal{N}}
.
We define the choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Chor}{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}\right)}}
by case analysis on the bottom-most rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
as shown in
Figure

15
.
We conclude by showing that
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Chor}{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}\right)}}\right)=P
by induction
on the structure of
\mathcal{D}
reasoning on the bottom-most rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
.
The rigth-to-left implication follows by
Theorem

4.1
.

Remark 10

Note that the statement could be made stronger by requiring the choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
to be flat. In this case, the proof of the right-to-left implication can be proven directly using the inverse of the translation in
Figure

15
.

Since every flat process in the
\pi
-calculus can be decorated with process names, we can easily extend the completeness result to the
\pi
-calculus.
We need to pay attention to the difference that
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
(instead of
P=\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
) because of the definition of
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}\right)
.
For example, the choreography that captures
\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}
, and
\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;\mathsf{Nil}\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}\right)=\mathsf{Nil}
.

Corollary 2

Every race-free and deadlock-free flat process
P
admits a choreography
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
such that
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
.

An important consequence of our results is that the processes that can be captured by choreographies can have cyclic dependencies, as we exemplified with
Equations

1
and
8
.
This significantly extends the proven expressivity of choreographic languages with name mobility, which so far have been shown to capture only the acyclic processes typable with linear logic
[
26
,
21
]
.

5

Figure 16:

Summary of key results in the literature.
We describe each column in order: the term constructors for restriction and parallel are separate (independent) in the syntax of processes; cyclic dependencies are allowed; processes can interact (communicate) on a free name (the name does not need to be restricted in the context); choreographies are proven complete for a class of processes (
N/A
means that this was not considered).

We now report on relevant related work. A summarising table of the differences between our work and others based on logic is given in
Figure

16
.

Proofs as Processes: Linear Logic and Session Types.

The proofs-as-processes agenda investigates how linear logic
[
44
]
can be used to reason about the behaviour of concurrent processes
[
1
,
17
]
.
It has inspired a number of works that aim at preventing safety issues, like processes performing incompatible actions in erroneous attempts to interact (e.g., sending a message with the wrong type).
Notable examples include
session types

[
57
,
58
]
and linear types for the
\pi
-calculus
[
65
]
. The former can actually be encoded into the latter – a formal reminder of their joint source of inspiration
[
37
]
.

A more recent line of research formally interprets propositions and proofs in linear logic as, respectively, session types and processes
[
20
,
99
]
.
This proofs-as-processes correspondence based on linear logic works for race-free processes, as we consider here.
However, it also presents some limitations compared to our framework.
Parallel composition and restriction are not offered as independent operators, because of a misalignment with the structures given by the standard rules of linear logic. For example, the
\mathsf{cut}
rule in linear logic handles both parallel composition and hiding, yielding a ‘fused’ restriction-parallel operator
(\nu x)(-\;|\;-)
.
Also, the
\otimes
rule for typing output has two premises, yielding another fused output-parallel operator
x!(y).(-\;|\;-)
– note that only bound names can be sent, as in the internal
\pi
-calculus
[
90
]
.
In particular, interaction between processes does not arise simply from parallel composition as in the standard
\pi
-calculus, but rather requires both parallel composition and restricting all names on which communication can take place (so communication is always an internal action).
This syntactic and semantic gap prevents linear logic from typing safe cyclic dependencies among processes, as in this simplification of
Equation

1
:

(\nu x)(\nu y)\left(x!\langle a\rangle.y?(b).\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(a).y!\langle b\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\right)

(12)

The same gap prevents having communication on unrestricted channels (as in
x!\langle a\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\;|\;x?(a).\mathsf{Nil}
) and having a private channel used by more than two processes.
Using
\invamp
and
\mathop{\otimes}
to type input and output is also in tension with the associativity and commutativity isomorphisms that come with these connectives. These isomorphisms yield unexpected equivalences at the process level, like
x?(a).y?(b).\mathsf{Nil}\equiv y?(b).x?(a).\mathsf{Nil}
.

These shortcomings are not present in our approach, thanks to the use of:

1.

The connective
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
for prefixing. The latter then has the expected ‘rigid’ non-commutative and non-associative semantics.

2.

The connective
\invamp
for parallelism. The latter then has the expected equivalences supported by the isomorphisms for
\invamp
.

3.

Nominal quantifiers, which allow for restricting names without imposing artificial constraints on the structure of processes.

While it is not the first time that these limitations are pointed out, our method is the first logical approach that overcomes them without ad-hoc machinery.
Previous works have introduced additional structures to linear logic, like hyperenvironments or indexed families of connectives, in order to address some of these issues
[
36
,
95
,
86
,
66
,
24
,
27
]
.
These additional structures are not necessary to our approach.

Choreographic Programming.

Choreographic programming was introduced in
[
80
]
as a paradigm for simplifying concurrent and distributed programming.
Crucial to the success of this paradigm is building choreographic programming languages that are expressive enough to capture as many safe concurrent behaviours as possible
[
81
]
.
However, most of the work conducted so far on the study of such expressivity is driven by applications, and a systematic understanding of the classes of processes that can be captured in choreographies is still relatively green.

In
[
31
,
63
]
the authors present methods for
choreography extraction
– inferring from a network of processes an equivalent choreography – for an asynchronous process calculus, respectively without and with process spawning.
Another purely algorithmic extraction procedure is provided in
[
69
]
, for simple choreographies without data – global types, which are roughly the choreographic equivalent of session types.
In linear logic, extracting global types from session types can be achieved via derivable rules
[
24
]
.

The only previous completeness result for the expressivity of choreographic programming is given in
[
26
,
21
]
, where it is shown that choreographies can capture the behaviours of all well-typed processes in linear logic.
Our work extends the completeness of choreographies to processes that, notably, can (i) have cyclic dependencies (like
Equation

12
), (ii) perform communication over free channels, (iii) respect the sequentiality of prefixing.
Moreover, and similarly to our previous discussion for proofs-as-processes, extraction in
[
26
,
21
]
requires additional structures (hypersequents and modalities to represent connections) that are not necessary in our method.

Non-Commutative Logic and Nominal Quantifiers.

Guglielmi proposed in
[
46
,
47
]
an extension of multiplicative linear logic with a non-commutative operator modelling the interaction of parallel and sequential operators.
This led to the design of the
calculus of structures

[
48
]
, a formalism for proofs where inference rules can be applied at any depth inside a formula rather than at the top-level connective, and the logic
\mathsf{BV}
including the (associative) non-commutative self-dual connective
\triangleleft
to model sequentiality.
In
[
19
]
Bruscoli has established a computation-as-deduction correspondence between specific derivations in
\mathsf{BV}
and executions in a simple fragment of
CCS
.
This correspondence has been extended in the works of Horne, Tiu et al. to include the choice operator (
+
) of Milner’s
CCS
(modelled via the additive connective
\mathop{\oplus}
), as well as the restriction to model private channels in the
\pi
-calculus
[
60
,
61
]
.
In these works, restriction has been modelled via
nominal quantifiers
in the spirit of the ones introduced by Pitts and Gabbay for
nominal logic

[
88
,
42
]
, by considering a pair of dual quantifiers
12
12

12

In
[
59
]
the authors report the use of a non-self-dual quantifier to model restriction was suggested them by Alessio Guglielmi in a private communication.
As explained in detail in
[
8
]
, the pair of dual nominal quantifiers in
[
59
,
61
,
60
]
is not the same pair we consider in this paper.
This can be observed by looking at the implication
\left(\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\!\left(A\right)\mathop{\otimes}\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\!\left(B\right)\right)\multimap\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}{x}.\!\left(A\invamp B\right)
, which is valid in these works, but it is not valid in
\mathsf{PiL}
.
For this reason we adopted a different symbol for the dual quantifier of
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
– i.e., our
\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}
instead of their
\mathord{\text{\char 221\relax}}
.

,
instead of a single self-dual quantifier as in
[
71
,
89
,
76
]
.

The logic
\mathsf{PiL}
we use as logical framework to establish our correspondences in this paper takes inspiration from Bruscoli’s work and its extension, but it uses a non-associative non-commutative self-dual connective
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
instead of the
\triangleleft
from
\mathsf{BV}
.
This seemingly irrelevant difference (the non-associativity of
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
) guarantees the existence of a
\mathsf{cut}
-free sequent calculus to be used as a framework for our correspondence, while for the logic
\mathsf{BV}
and its extension
\mathsf{cut}
-free sequent calculi cannot exist, as proven in
[
94
]
.
Note that requiring non-associativity for the connective modelling sequentiality is not a syntactical stretch, because the same restriction naturally occurs in process calculi such as
CCS
and the
\pi
-calculus, where sequentiality is defined by an asymmetric prefix operation only allowing to sequentially compose (on the left) atomic instructions, such as send and receive.
The other main difference is that in these works derivations represent a single execution, while our derivations represent execution trees.
This allows us to state our
Theorem

3.1
without quantifying on the set of derivations of
\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.

6
Conclusion and Future Works

We presented a new approach to the study of processes based on logic, which leverages an interpretation of processes in the
\pi
-calculus as formulas in the proof system
\mathsf{PiL}
.
By seeing derivations as computation trees, we obtained an elegant method to reason about deadlock-freedom that goes beyond the syntactic and semantic limitations of previous work based on logic.
This led us to establishing the first completeness result for the expressivity of choreographic programming with respect to mobile processes with cyclic dependencies.

We discuss next some interesting future directions.

Recursion.
Recursion could be modelled by extending
\mathsf{PiL}
with fixpoint operators and rules like the ones in
[
15
,
14
,
3
,
4
,
9
]
.
We foresee no major challenges in extending the proof of cut-elimination for
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
to
\mathsf{PiL}
,
since the behaviour of the connective
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}
is purely multiplicative (in the sense of
[
35
,
45
,
6
]
) and the rules for nominal quantifiers do not require the employment of new techniques.
In
\mathsf{PiL}
with recursion, properties such as
justness
or
fairness
could be characterised by specific constraints on derivations, corresponding to constraints on threads and paths of the (possibly cyclic) execution trees.

Asynchronous
\pi
-calculus.
We foresee the possibility of modelling asynchronous communication by including shared buffers, inspired by previous work on concurrent constraint programming
[
91
,
85
]
and its strong ties to logic programming
[
79
,
92
,
62
,
40
,
83
,
84
,
87
]
.
However, buffers with capacity greater than
2
have non-sequential-parallel structures and therefore cannot be described efficiently using binary connectives
[
97
,
30
]
.
We may thus need to consider
graphical connectives

[
2
,
5
]
.

Proof Nets.

In
[
8
]
we define proof nets for
\mathsf{PiL}
, capturing local rule permutations, and providing canonical representative for execution trees up-to interleaving concurrency.
This syntax could be used to
refine the correspondence between proofs and choreographies (
Theorem

4.3
).
We plan to study the extension of the computation-as-deduction paradigm in the case of proof net expansion, following the ideas in
[
11
,
12
,
7
]
, as well as to use a notion of orthogonality for modules of proof nets (in the sense of
[
35
,
12
,
7
]
) to study testing preorders
[
38
,
39
,
51
,
18
]
.

Completeness of Choreographies.

The literature of choreographic programming languages includes features of practical interest that extend the expressivity of choreographies – like process spawning
[
32
]
and nondeterminism
[
81
]
.
Exploring extensions of
\mathsf{PiL}
to capture these features is interesting future work.
For process spawning, a simple solution could be achieved by defining a way to dynamically assign process names to properly define the map
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Chor}{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\right)}}
.

Additional Proofs

x!\langle w\rangle.P\left[x/y\right]&\mbox{ if }y=z\\
z!\langle x\rangle.P\left[x/y\right]&\mbox{ if }y=w\end{cases}\\

z?(w).P&\mbox{ if }y=w\end{cases}\\

x\triangleright\left\{\ell:P_{\ell}\left[x/y\right]\right\}_{\ell\in L}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

Figure 17:
Notation for substitution

See
1

Proof

If
P\equiv\mathsf{Nil}
then
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
is maximal.
Otherwise, we assume there is a leaf
P^{\prime}\not\equiv
of
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
.
Since
P
is deadlock-free, then there is a process
Q
such that
P^{\prime}\to Q
.
If the core reduction of
P^{\prime}\to Q
is a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Label}
, we extend
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
grafting
Q
in
P^{\prime}
.
If the core reduction of
P^{\prime}\to Q
is a
\mathsf{Choice}
, then there is a set
\{Q_{\ell}\mid\ell\in L\}\ni Q
such that

P^{\prime}\to Q_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in L
. In this case
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
can be extended grafting the processes in
\{Q_{\ell}\mid\ell\in L\}
in
P^{\prime}
.
Since this reasoning can be iterated on any leaf
P\not\equiv\mathsf{Nil}
, we conclude because no branch of the execution tree of a process (without recursion) can be infinite.

See
2.2

Proof

By induction on the structure of the contexts.

1.

If
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}A\multimap B
and
\mathcal{C}[\bullet]=[\bullet]
the claim is trivial.
Otherwise, we have a derivation starting with a
\invamp
-rule and continuing as follows:

•

If
\mathcal{C}[\bullet]\in\{\mathcal{C}_{1}[\bullet]\invamp D,\mathcal{C}_{1}[\bullet]\mathop{\otimes}D\}
,
we apply a
\invamp
-rule followed by a
\mathop{\otimes}
rule splitting the context so that one of premise is
\vdash{\mathcal{C}_{1}[A]}^{\bot},\mathcal{C}_{1}[B]
and the other is
\vdash D,{D}^{\bot}
.
The former is provable by inductive hypothesis, the last by
Theorem

\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}\mathchar 45\relax\mathord{\text{\char 223\relax}}

We conclude by inductive hypothesis.

To prove

,

. Therefore, there is a set of processes

),

.

\mathsf{Core}_{(S,S^{\prime})}=\mathsf{Core}_{(P,P^{\prime})}

\mathsf{Com}
(resp.
\mathsf{Label}
),

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left[\!\left[S^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[S\right]\!\right]

.

.
1
;

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{PiL}}\,}\left(\bigwith_{\ell\in L}\left[\!\left[S_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)\multimap\left[\!\left[S\right]\!\right]

Proof

To prove the theorem we show a correspondence between maximal execution trees and derivations in
\mathsf{PiL}
.

(
\Rightarrow
)

If
P
is deadlock-free, then, by
Lemma

1
, any maximal execution tree
\mathsf{Ctree}(P)
with root
P
has leaves which are processes structurally equivalent to
\mathsf{Nil}
.
We reason on the structure of any such tree
\mathcal{T}
.

•

If the root
P
is a leaf, then
P\Rrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\Rrightarrow\mathsf{Nil}
because
P
is deadlock-free.
Then, by
Lemma

2
.
1
there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}
of
\vdash\circ\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
.
We conclude by applying cut-elimination (
Theorem

2.1
.
2
) to the derivation made by composing (via
\mathsf{cut}
-rule) the derivation
\mathcal{D}
with the single-rule derivation made of a
\circ
.

•

If
P
has a unique child
P^{\prime}
and the core-reduction of
P\to P^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{Com}
or a
\mathsf{Label}
, then there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}
of
\vdash\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
by
Lemma

2
.
We conclude by applying cut-elimination (
Theorem

2.1
.
2
) to the derivation made by composing (via
\mathsf{cut}
-rule) the derivation
\mathcal{D}
with the derivation with conclusion
\left[\!\left[P^{\prime}\right]\!\right]
, which exists by inductive hypothesis.

•

Otherwise,
P
has set of children
\{P_{\ell}\mid\ell\in L\}
and the core-reduction of
P\to P_{\ell}
is a
\mathsf{Choice}
for each
\ell\in L
.
Then there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}
of
\vdash\bigwith_{\ell\in L}\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\multimap\left[\!\left[P\right]\!\right]
by
Lemma

2
.
We conclude by applying cut-elimination (
Theorem

2.1
.
2
) to the derivation made by composing (via
\mathsf{cut}
-rule) the derivation
\mathcal{D}
with the derivation starting with a
\with
-rule with premises the conclusion of a derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
with conclusion
\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]
, which exists by inductive hypothesis.

\Leftarrow
)

To prove the converse, we show that each derivation

(13)

Therefore, we can transform
\mathcal{D}_{P}
into a derivation of the shape above in the center, where the derivations
\mathcal{D}^{-}
are made of
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
- and
\invamp
-rules only.
We can conclude by inductive hypothesis on the derivation in the right of
Equation

13
, since it provides a derivation in
\mathsf{PiL}
for the
{P^{\prime}}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\left[Q\;|\;R\left[y/z\right]\right]
, where
P^{\prime}
is the process such that
P\to P^{\prime}
via a reduction step with core-reduction a
\mathsf{Com}
.

2.

Otherwise, we can deduce that
P=\mathcal{N}\left[x\triangleright\left\{\ell:Q_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L},x\triangleleft\left\{\ell:R_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\right]
,
then we can apply a similar reasoning to deduce that
\mathcal{D}_{P}
is of the following shape below on the left.

1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 29.20467pt}\kern-1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 29.20467pt\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}\kern 29.20467pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 24.05283pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vdash\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]$}}\kern 16.43202pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\}_{\ell\in L}\end{array}\\
\mbox{
with
$\alpha=\bigoplus\limits_{\ell\in L_{1}}{\left((x\wn\ell)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[Q_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)}$
and
$\beta=\bigwith\limits_{\ell\in L_{2}}{\left(\langle x\oc\ell\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{720897}{396393}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{610759}{285425}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}{\hbox{\resizebox{550688}{216600}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.0mu\blacktriangleleft$}}}}}\left[\!\left[R_{\ell}\right]\!\right]\right)}$
,
and
with $\alpha^{\prime}=\left[\!\left[Q_{\ell}\right]\!\right]$
and $\beta^{\prime}=\left[\!\left[R_{\ell}\right]\!\right]$
}\end{array}

(14)

Then we can apply rule permutation to obtain a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{P}
of the shape above in the center,
where the derivations
\mathcal{D}^{-}
are made of
\mathord{\text{\char 200\relax}}
- and
\invamp
-rules only.
We again conclude by inductive hypothesis since
there is a derivation of the formula

\left[\!\left[P_{\ell}\right]\!\right]=\left[\!\left[\mathcal{N}\left[Q_{\ell}\;|\;R_{\ell}\right]\right]\!\right]

as shown above in the right of
Equation

14

and

P\to P_{\ell}
.

Note that since
P
is race-free, then it suffices to reason on a single execution tree and not to take into account all possible execution trees of
P
.

Lemma 4

Let
P
,
Q
,
R
and
S
be endpoint processes.

1.

For all
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
,

(P\;|\;Q)\sqsupseteq(P^{\prime}\;|\;Q^{\prime})\iff P\sqsupseteq P^{\prime}\mbox{ and }Q\sqsupseteq Q^{\prime}\;.

.

2.

If
P\sqsupseteq Q
,
then
x?(y).P\sqsupseteq x?(y).Q
and
x!\langle y\rangle.P\sqsupseteq x!\langle y\rangle.Q
.

3.

If
P\sqsupseteq Q
and
P\sqsupseteq R
,
then
P\sqsupseteq(Q\sqcup R)
.

4.

If
P\sqsupseteq Q
and
R\sqsupseteq S
,
then
(P\sqcup R)\sqsupseteq(Q\sqcup S)
.

Proof

These standard properties of merge are proven in Chapter
8
of
[
81
]
.

Lemma 5

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
be a flat projectable choreography.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is projectable and
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\sqsupseteq\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)

for all
\mathsf{r}\notin\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu})
.

Proof

By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]
.
Since substitution does not affect
\mathsf{r}\notin\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}}\}
, we conclude by definition of EPP observing that

\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)=\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]\right)
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Choice}}
, then there is a
\ell_{i}\in L
such that

\scalebox{0.9068022366860704}[0.9068022366860704]{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{${\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}:k}\;\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{i}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{C}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}$}}}}}}\;.

We conclude since

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\sqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)=\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Label}}
, then
\ell_{i}\in L^{\prime}

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{i}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{C}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathsf{p}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}{\mathsf{q}}:k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\;.

We conclude since
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\sqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)\sqsupseteq\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Com}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}

for some choreographies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}
with

\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu})\cap\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}},k\}=\varnothing
.
We have the following cases:

–

If
{\mathsf{q}}\neq\mathsf{r}\neq{\mathsf{p}}
, then

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)
and
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}\right)

. We conclude by inductive hypothesis.

–

If
\mathsf{r}={\mathsf{p}}
, then

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=k!\langle x\rangle.\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)

and
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=k!\langle x\rangle.\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}\right)
.
We conclude by inductive hypothesis and
Item

2
.

–

Otherwise,
\mathsf{r}={\mathsf{q}}
and

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=k?(x).\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)

and
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=k?(x).\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{2}\right)
.
We conclude by inductive hypothesis and
Item

2
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax Choice}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:C_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}\xrightarrow{\;\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L^{\prime}}:k\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}_{\ell}&\ell\in L\\
\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}

for some choreographies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}_{\ell}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}^{1}
with
\mathsf{pn}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu})\cap\{{\mathsf{p}},{\mathsf{q}},k\}=\varnothing
for all
\ell\in L
.
We have the following cases:

–

If
{\mathsf{q}}\neq\mathsf{r}\neq{\mathsf{p}}
, then
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\sqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)
and

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=\sqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}_{\ell}\right)
.
We conclude by inductive hypothesis and
Item

4
.

–

If
\mathsf{r}={\mathsf{p}}
, then

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=k\triangleleft\left\{\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)\right\}_{\ell\in L}

and

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=k\triangleleft\left\{\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}_{\ell}\right)\right\}_{\ell\in L}
.
We conclude by inductive hypothesis.

–

If
\mathsf{r}={\mathsf{q}}
then

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\
\ell:S_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}

and

\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\
\ell:S_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}\;.

We conclude by inductive hypothesis.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Rest}}
the claim follows by inductive hypothesis.

Proof

Completeness
.
By case analysis on the reduction steps.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is obtained by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Com}}
step, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{x}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{y}:k};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]
.
By definition

\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k!\langle x\rangle.\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k?(y).\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)

and by
Lemma

5
we also have that

\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]\right)\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]\right)\;.

Since
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]\right)=\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)

and

\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\right)\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]=\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{1}\left[x/y_{{\mathsf{q}}}\right]\right)

we conclude observing that
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)
by a
\mathsf{E}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Com}
step.

\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}
.

, we have that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=Q\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)\right\}_{\ell\in L}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\

\mbox{and}\\
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)=Q\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{i}:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell_{i}}\right)\right\}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\
\ell:S_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

with

.
Hence,
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\xrightarrow{\;{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}\;}\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}\right)

\ell:{S}_{\ell}&\ell\in L^{\prime}\setminus L\end{array}\right\}}
.
By definition

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)=Q\;|\;{\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{i}:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell_{i}}\right)\right\}\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon k\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l|@{\;}l}\ell:\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{\ell}\right)&\ell\in L\\

Soundness
. By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
:

•

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{0}}
then
P=\mathsf{Nil}
and there is no transition to consider;

•

if

Proof

If

Appendix 0.B
Running example of choreography extraction

Consider the following choreographies.

\begin{array}[]{r@{=}l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{L}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{L}:k_{{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{q}}}}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell_{1}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{q}.{x_{1}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{r}.{a}:k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}\}}:k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell_{1}^{\prime}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{q}.{y_{1}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{p}.{z}:k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}}\end{array}\right\}}\\
\ell_{2}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{q}.{x_{2}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{r}.{a}:k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{p}.{\{\ell_{2}^{\prime}\}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{q}.{\{\ell_{2}^{\prime}\}}:k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}}{\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell_{2}^{\prime}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{q}.{y_{1}}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{p}.{z}:k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}}\end{array}\right\}}\end{array}\right\}}\\

(22)

We observe that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}^{\prime}
is not projectable, and that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
can be obtained from

[

,

,
34
]
.
The endpoint projection of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
is defined as follows.

\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)={\mathsf{p}}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\;|\;{\mathsf{q}}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)\;|\;\mathsf{r}\colon\!\!\!\colon\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)

with

\begin{array}[]{l@{=}l}\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{p}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&k_{{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{q}}}\triangleleft\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\colon}l}\ell_{1}&k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}?(z).\mathsf{Nil},\\
\ell_{2}&k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}?(z).\mathsf{Nil}\end{array}\right\}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{{\mathsf{q}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&k_{{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{q}}}\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\colon}l}\ell_{1}&k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}!\langle x_{1}\rangle.k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{1}^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{Nil}\right\},\\
\ell_{2}&k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}!\langle x_{2}\rangle.k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}\triangleleft\left\{\ell_{2}^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{Nil}\right\}\end{array}\right\}\\
\mathsf{EPP}_{\mathsf{r}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)&k_{{\mathsf{q}}\mathsf{r}}?(a).k_{{\mathsf{p}}{\mathsf{q}}}\triangleright\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\colon}l}\ell^{\prime}_{1}&k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}!\langle y_{1}\rangle.\mathsf{Nil},\\
\ell^{\prime}_{2}&k_{{\mathsf{p}}\mathsf{r}}!\langle y_{1}\rangle.\mathsf{Nil}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

The process
\mathsf{EPP}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right)
is derivable in
